---


# Studies: Impulse Control Disorders — Treatment (6.3.3)

> Chapter 6.3.3 covers two main treatment types for impulse control disorders (kleptomania, pyromania, gambling disorder):
> **Biological** (opiate antagonists) and **Psychological** (covert sensitisation, imaginal desensitisation).
> Three formal studies / focus cases are covered: Grant et al. (2008), Glover (1985), and Blaszczynski & Nower (2003).

---

## Background: The Biology You Need First

Before the studies make sense, you need to understand the **opioid-dopamine reward loop**. Here it is as a chain:

| Step | What happens |
|------|-------------|
| 1 | You gamble / steal / start a fire |
| 2 | Brain releases **endorphins** (natural opioids) |
| 3 | Endorphins bind to **opioid receptors** |
| 4 | Opioid receptors suppress **GABA** |
| 5 | Less GABA = less inhibition of **dopamine** |
| 6 | Dopamine floods the reward system → **euphoria** |
| 7 | Euphoria reinforces the behaviour (operant conditioning) |

> 💡 **Opiate antagonists** (e.g., nalmefene, naltrexone) **block opioid receptors**. No receptor activation = no GABA suppression = no dopamine spike = no euphoria = no reward = behaviour fades.

---

---

## Study 1: Grant et al. (2008) — Predicting who responds to opiate antagonists for gambling disorder

### [Grant et al. (2008)] — Which gamblers benefit most from opiate-blocker drugs?

---

**1. Why this study exists**

Gambling disorder was known to involve the opioid-dopamine reward system, and drugs like **nalmefene** and **naltrexone** had already been trialled as treatments.
But doctors had a problem: not everyone responds to these drugs.
Some people take them and improve dramatically. Others see barely any effect.

Grant et al. wanted to figure out: **who is most likely to benefit?**

If you can predict who will respond, you can give the right drug to the right person rather than wasting time and money on treatments that won't work.

> 🧠 Think of it like asking: "Before we prescribe chemotherapy, which cancer patients are most likely to respond?" — personalised medicine logic.

---

**2. The procedure — told as a story**

Imagine a clinic waiting room. 284 Americans walk in, all diagnosed with **pathological gambling** (DSM-IV) and scoring 5+ on the **South Oaks Gambling Screen (SOGS)** — a validated scale for gambling severity.

They are split into two separate drug trials happening at the same time:

- **Trial 1:** 207 outpatients from 15 psychiatric centres across the USA. They will take **nalmefene** (or placebo) every day for **16 weeks**.
- **Trial 2:** 77 participants from one centre in Minnesota. They will take **naltrexone** (or placebo) for **18 weeks**.

**Within each trial**, participants are randomly assigned to one of four groups:

| Group | What they receive |
|-------|--------------------|
| Placebo | Sugar pill — no active drug |
| Low dose | Nalmefene 25 mg OR Naltrexone 50 mg |
| Medium dose | Nalmefene 50 mg OR Naltrexone 100 mg |
| High dose | Nalmefene 100 mg OR Naltrexone 150 mg |

The study is **double-blind**: neither the participants nor the researchers measuring outcomes know who got the real drug.
This is critical — if the researcher knew you had the drug, their voice, eye contact, or tone of questioning could subtly shift, inflating how much "progress" they detect.

> 💡 Think of a blind taste test. If the judge knows which glass is the expensive wine, they will likely rate it higher — even subconsciously. Double-blinding removes this bias entirely.

**What they measured (before and after treatment):**

- Severity of gambling symptoms: a modified **Yale-Brown Obsessive-Compulsive Scale (Y-BOCS)** — a clinically validated questionnaire
- A drop of >35% in Y-BOCS score = **positive response to treatment**
- Daily functioning, anxiety, depression (questionnaires)
- Family history of alcoholism in first-degree relatives (semi-structured interview)
- Type of gambling (strategic like poker vs non-strategic like slots)
- Comorbid diagnoses (structured clinical interview)

After the 16/18 weeks, the two datasets were analysed separately — but the results were so similar the researchers **combined them** to increase statistical power.

---

**3. What they found**

**Who responded to the real drug?**

| Predictor | Strength of association | Finding |
|-----------|------------------------|---------|
| **Family history of alcoholism** (first-degree relative) | **Strongest** (p = 0.006) | Robustly predicted positive response to opiate antagonists |
| **Intensity of gambling urges at baseline** | Mild trend (higher doses only) | Stronger urges slightly predicted better response to drug |
| Age | Predicted placebo response, not drug response | Younger participants were 30% more likely to respond to placebo per decade younger |

**Demographics of note:**
- Average age of onset: **29 years old**
- Average gap from starting gambling to diagnosis: **11 years** (range: 1–40 years)
- Only **19%** had previously sought professional help
- **30%** had attended Gamblers Anonymous
- **42%** were regular tobacco users
- **24%** had a comorbid mood disorder

> ⚡ The single most important finding: **family history of alcoholism in a first-degree relative is the strongest predictor of a positive response to opiate antagonists.**

---

**4. Why it matters**

This study has direct **clinical application**.

Before Grant et al. (2008), doctors choosing between opiate antagonists and other treatments (antidepressants, antipsychotics) were essentially guessing.

Now, a doctor can ask two quick questions:
1. Does anyone in your immediate family have alcoholism?
2. How strong are your urges to gamble?

These answers — cheap and fast to obtain — significantly improve the probability of selecting the right drug.

> 🧠 It also explains **why placebos work more for younger patients**: younger people may be more susceptible to the belief that they are being treated, which itself reduces gambling behaviour. This is why placebos must always be controlled for.

---

**5. Strengths and Weaknesses**

**Strengths:**

✅ **Double-blind, placebo-controlled design**
→ WHY: Neither participant nor researcher knew who received real drug
→ CONSEQUENCE: Reduces experimenter bias and demand characteristics, increasing **internal validity**

✅ **Large, diverse sample for nalmefene trial (N = 207, 15 centres)**
→ WHY: Recruited from different geographic locations, ages 19–72, varied ethnicity and employment
→ CONSEQUENCE: Increases **generalisability** of nalmefene findings to the wider population of gambling disorder patients

✅ **Combined two RCTs (meta-analytic approach)**
→ WHY: The two drug results were so similar that combining them increased statistical power
→ CONSEQUENCE: More reliable conclusions about predictors of treatment response

---

**Weaknesses:**

❌ **Family history of alcoholism was self-reported**
→ WHY: Participants may not know all their relatives' medical histories (deceased relatives, adoption)
→ CONSEQUENCE: This key predictor variable may be **inaccurate**, which could over- or underestimate its importance; hard to replicate because semi-structured interviews vary between interviewers

❌ **No follow-up after the trial ended**
→ WHY: The study stopped measuring at week 16/18
→ CONSEQUENCE: We don't know if the gains lasted. Relapse is common in gambling disorder — if participants stopped taking medication after the trial, any improvement may have reversed. Long-term efficacy is unknown.

❌ **Naltrexone sample was small and non-diverse (N = 77, 90% Caucasian, one location: Minnesota)**
→ WHY: Smaller, less varied sample
→ CONSEQUENCE: The naltrexone-specific findings cannot be generalised as confidently as the nalmefene findings

❌ **Reductionist approach**
→ WHY: Focuses solely on blocking neurochemical reward; does not address underlying beliefs, coping skills, or emotional regulation
→ CONSEQUENCE: Without psychological therapy alongside medication, underpinning cognitive patterns remain unchanged. Once the drug stops, relapse is likely because the person hasn't developed alternative coping strategies.

---

**6. Exam tips**

⚡ **Must-knows:**
- Meta-analysis combining **two RCTs** (nalmefene 16-week + naltrexone 18-week)
- **Double-blind, placebo-controlled, independent measures**
- N = 284; diagnosed with DSM-IV pathological gambling + SOGS ≥ 5
- Main finding: **family history of alcoholism** = strongest predictor
- Secondary: **strong urges** (higher doses only) = mild predictor
- Placebo finding: **younger participants** more susceptible to placebo
- No follow-up = weakness
- Self-reported family history = validity weakness

📋 **Skeleton answer (6-mark describe):**
> Grant et al. (2008) combined data from two RCTs into a meta-analysis to investigate predictors of response to opiate antagonists in gambling disorder. 284 American outpatients diagnosed using DSM-IV were randomly allocated to placebo or low/medium/high doses of either nalmefene (16 weeks) or naltrexone (18 weeks). The study was double-blind and used the Y-BOCS to measure gambling symptom severity. A 35%+ reduction in Y-BOCS score was classified as a positive response. The strongest predictor of a positive drug response was family history of alcoholism. Strength of gambling urges also showed a mild association with positive response at higher doses.

📋 **Skeleton answer (eval — application to everyday life):**
> A strength of this study is its direct clinical application. By identifying family history of alcoholism as the key predictor, clinicians can ask a simple, low-cost screening question before prescribing. However, self-reporting of family history reduces validity, as participants may lack accurate knowledge of relatives' diagnoses. The lack of follow-up data is also a significant weakness, as relapse is common in gambling disorder and long-term efficacy cannot be established from this study.

---

---

## Study 2: Glover (1985) — Covert sensitisation for kleptomania

### [Glover (1985)] — Teaching a woman's brain to feel disgusted by the urge to steal

---

**1. Why this study exists**

By 1985, the dominant behavioural approach assumed that **all non-reflex behaviours are learned — and therefore can be unlearned**.

A woman had been stealing daily for 14 years. Standard psychiatric approaches (antidepressants, imagining arrest) had not worked.
Glover used a technique called **covert sensitisation** — pairing mental images of the unwanted behaviour with intensely unpleasant imagined consequences — to try to extinguish the impulse.

This case study was important because it demonstrated the technique could work even for a deeply entrenched, decades-long pattern of behaviour.

---

**2. The procedure — told as a story**

Meet the client: a **56-year-old married woman**. She had been stealing from supermarkets every single lunchtime for 14 years.

It started a year after her husband was convicted of embezzlement. She felt ashamed, her social circle disappeared, she took extra work to support them, and the resentment built. Somewhere in that shame spiral, a daily stealing habit took root.

She stole things she didn't need — baby shoes, random items. She described the urge as "overwhelming." She wished she were "chained to a wall" to stop herself.

She had already tried imagining being arrested and prosecuted. It hadn't worked.

**So Glover tried something different: vomiting imagery.**

**The therapy — 4 sessions, once every 2 weeks:**

| Session | What happened |
|---------|--------------|
| 1–2 | Muscle-relaxing medication used to deepen relaxation; therapist guided her to vividly imagine approaching items in a shop as if to steal, then imagine herself vomiting violently; other shoppers stare; she feels humiliated |
| 1–2 (crucial moment) | She imagines replacing the item. The vomiting stops. Relief floods in. The "not stealing" becomes associated with relief. |
| 3–4 | Self-hypnosis replaced the medication; she felt this made her visualisations more vivid and powerful |

> 💡 This is **classical conditioning** in action:
> - Unconditioned stimulus (vomiting image) → Unconditioned response (disgust/nausea)
> - Paired repeatedly with: thought of stealing (conditioned stimulus)
> - Result: thought of stealing → conditioned response (disgust) — the urge is now aversive

**Homework:** She practised the visualisation exercises **several times a day**.
**Practical rule:** She was told never to shop without a written list, and to leave the bag she used for stealing at home.

**Follow-up sessions:** Once every 3 months after the initial 2 months of treatment.

---

**3. What they found**

▼ **Progress over 19 months:**

| Timepoint | What happened |
|-----------|--------------|
| After 4 sessions (2 months) | Urges reduced; stole on 2 occasions (5 low-value items from 4 shops) — massively better than daily stealing |
| 3-month follow-up | Continuing daily homework; growing confidence to shop alone |
| 9-month follow-up | One lapse: stole a bar of soap; but it "no longer relieved tension as it once would have done" |
| 19-month follow-up | **No further relapses**; more cheerful, confident, sociable; rarely thought about stealing; if she did, the thought made her feel unwell rather than compelled |

> 🧠 The 9-month lapse is important: even after a relapse, the behaviour had lost its reinforcing power. The conditioned disgust had weakened the reward.

---

**4. Why it matters**

This case showed that a **14-year daily compulsion** could be significantly reduced in **just 4 sessions across 2 months** — and the gains lasted at least 19 months.

It also demonstrated that therapy must be personalised. When arrest imagery failed, vomiting imagery succeeded. The patient's own preference mattered.

It supports the **behaviourist position** that impulse control disorders are learned habits that can be extinguished using conditioning principles — without medication.

Kohn and Antonuccio (2002) later replicated success with a similar approach in a **39-year-old male** who had been stealing since age 6, showing the technique works across gender and age of onset.

---

**5. Strengths and Weaknesses**

**Strengths:**

✅ **Rich, detailed idiographic data**
→ WHY: The full case history (client's background, husband's crime, failed prior therapies) allows readers to understand why this approach worked for this specific client
→ CONSEQUENCE: Helps other therapists judge how transferable the findings are to their own clients — genuine clinical utility

✅ **Long follow-up period (19 months)**
→ WHY: Unlike many therapy studies, Glover tracked the client for over a year and a half post-treatment
→ CONSEQUENCE: Evidence of durable, not just short-term, behaviour change

✅ **No side effects**
→ WHY: Unlike opiate antagonists, covert sensitisation involves no medication
→ CONSEQUENCE: No risk of nausea, dizziness, dysphoria, or other drug side effects that cause patients to stop treatment

---

**Weaknesses:**

❌ **Single case study — N = 1**
→ WHY: Only one participant (a 56-year-old woman with a very specific life context)
→ CONSEQUENCE: Cannot generalise to the broader population of people with kleptomania — low **external validity**

❌ **Relies on vivid imagery ability**
→ WHY: The woman herself credited the therapy's success to her ability to vividly imagine the scenes
→ CONSEQUENCE: The therapy may be ineffective for people with **aphantasia** (inability to form mental images) or those with reduced imaginative capacity. Wicken et al. (2021) showed that people with aphantasia have flattened physiological responses to frightening scenarios — suggesting the conditioning mechanism would not work.

❌ **Cannot isolate the active ingredient**
→ WHY: The therapy combined vomiting imagery, muscle relaxation, self-hypnosis, practical strategies (shopping list, leaving the bag), and regular homework
→ CONSEQUENCE: Glover himself admits it is "impossible to know exactly which aspects were most effective" — the study lacks **internal validity** regarding mechanism

❌ **No control condition**
→ WHY: Case study design — no comparison group
→ CONSEQUENCE: We cannot rule out spontaneous remission or non-specific therapy effects (e.g., attention from a therapist). Without a control, causation cannot be established.

---

**6. Exam tips**

⚡ **Must-knows:**
- Client: 56-year-old married woman; 14 years of daily stealing; husband's embezzlement was the trigger
- 4 sessions over 2 months, then follow-up every 3 months
- Sessions 1–2: muscle relaxant + vomiting imagery
- Sessions 3–4: self-hypnosis + vomiting imagery
- Vomiting stops when she imagines REPLACING the item → relief conditioned to NOT stealing
- Homework: practice imagery multiple times daily; strict shopping list; leave the stealing bag at home
- 19-month follow-up: only 1 lapse (bar of soap at 9 months); vast improvement overall
- Based on **classical conditioning** / **evaluative learning**
- Weakness: N = 1, relies on imagery ability (aphantasia concern), cannot isolate cause

📋 **Skeleton answer (describe, 6 marks):**
> Glover (1985) treated a 56-year-old woman who had stolen daily for 14 years using covert sensitisation. The therapist guided her through visualising approaching items in a supermarket as if to steal, then vividly imagining herself vomiting in front of other shoppers. When she imagined replacing the items, the vomiting would stop, creating a sense of relief — conditioning relief to non-stealing. Sessions 1–2 used muscle relaxants to aid relaxation; sessions 3–4 used self-hypnosis. Homework involved practising the imagery several times daily. At 19-month follow-up, she had only one minor lapse and reported the urge no longer felt overwhelming.

📋 **Eval point on idiographic vs nomothetic:**
> Glover (1985) used an **idiographic approach**, collecting detailed qualitative data specific to one individual. This allowed the therapy to be tailored to the client's own preferences (vomiting over arrest imagery) and her specific life context. However, because the approach is idiographic, it cannot generate nomothetic (universal) conclusions about the efficacy of covert sensitisation across all people with kleptomania.

---

---

## Study 3: Blaszczynski & Nower (2003) — Imaginal desensitisation for gambling disorder

### [Blaszczynski & Nower (2003)] — Using relaxation and guided imagination to defuse gambling urges

---

**1. Why this study exists**

Covert sensitisation works by making the target behaviour feel disgusting.
**Imaginal desensitisation works the opposite way**: instead of punishing the urge, it teaches the person to experience the full chain of events leading up to the behaviour — **but feel calm rather than aroused throughout**, and ultimately walk away.

The theory is McConaghy's (1980) **Behaviour Completion Mechanism**: compulsive behaviours keep recurring because the person never mentally "completes" the behavioural sequence. The incompleteness creates tension, which drives the urge again. Imaginal desensitisation provides a full completion — but a calm, non-harmful one.

Blaszczynski and Nower (2003) wrote a practical guide to the technique using a real case study to illustrate exactly how a therapist should implement it.

---

**2. The procedure — told as a story**

Meet **Mary Doe**: a 52-year-old divorced American woman, mother of two, working as a bookkeeper in Missouri. She had grown up watching her grandmother play cards. She started gambling for money in school. When gambling was legalised in Missouri, she started going to a local casino after work — every single day.

She lost **$25,000**. She began imagining stealing money to fund her gambling.

**Step 1 — The initial interview.**
The therapist sits down with Mary and uses open questions to map her **gambling trigger chain** — the precise sequence of environmental and emotional cues that lead from the beginning of her day to sitting at the poker table. Not just "you feel like gambling" — every step: the time of day, the feeling of loneliness at 5 pm, the drive past the casino, the smell of popcorn and vanilla, her favourite dealer's smile.

**Step 2 — Writing the personalised script.**
The therapist uses this information to write a detailed ~20-minute script divided into **6 scenes**:

| Scene | Purpose | Mary's example |
|-------|---------|----------------|
| 1 | Trigger the urge | 5 pm at work, colleagues leaving, loneliness builds, urge to visit casino rises |
| 2 | Planning to act | She decides to "just drive past" — packs up, feels excited |
| 3 | Arriving at venue | Approaches casino entrance, sees lights, hears music, smells popcorn and vanilla |
| 4 | Building arousal | Sees her favourite dealer smiling; feels confident; plans her bet |
| 5 | "Second thoughts" | Looks around — other players look discouraged; remembers past losses; debts flood her mind; excitement drains |
| 6 | Walking away | Leaves the table; walks out of the casino; feels **relieved and proud**; goes to a cinema instead |

> 💡 Scenes 1–4 are vivid and accurate — they must generate real arousal or the technique won't work.
> Scenes 5–6 are the "turn" — this is where the therapist engineers the calm alternative ending.

**Step 3 — The relaxation framework.**
Every session starts with **4–5 minutes of Progressive Muscle Relaxation (PMR)**: tensing each muscle group as you breathe in, releasing as you breathe out.
Between each scene in the script, there are a few minutes of PMR to maintain deep relaxation.

> 💡 The logic: if you are deeply relaxed while imagining the trigger, your nervous system learns that the trigger does NOT mean arousal. The conditioned arousal response is extinguished.

**Step 4 — Homework.**
Mary is given an audio recording of the script.
She listens to it **2–3 times a day, for 5–7 days** — ideally at the same times each day (e.g., morning, late afternoon, before bed).
She tracks her sessions, urge strength, and any gambling incidents on handouts.

**Step 5 — Follow-up sessions.**
She may only have **2–3 face-to-face sessions** with the therapist:
1. Interview to gather information for the script
2. Practice PMR + run through the script; explain homework
3. Check progress; modify script if needed

---

**3. What they found**

> Important note: Blaszczynski and Nower (2003) used this case primarily as an **illustrative example** of how to conduct the therapy. They do **not report outcome data for Mary specifically**.

However, supporting evidence from other research:

| Study | Finding |
|-------|---------|
| **Grant et al. (2011)** | 80% of gambling disorder patients achieved abstinence after 6 sessions of **imaginal desensitisation + motivational interviewing (IDMI)**; 77% still abstinent at 6-month follow-up |
| **Grant et al. (2011) vs Gamblers Anonymous** | IDMI: 63.6% achieved abstinence; Gamblers Anonymous: only 17.1% |
| **McConaghy et al. (1991) long-term** | Imaginal desensitisation showed superior outcomes compared to aversion therapy at 2–9 year follow-up |
| **Small increase at 6-month follow-up** | Slight symptom return compared to immediate post-treatment — suggests maintenance sessions help |

---

**4. Why it matters**

Unlike medication, this therapy gives the client **transferable skills**.

The client learns PMR, which they can use in real life whenever a trigger appears — e.g., a radio advert for the lottery makes them feel the urge, and they can consciously deploy PMR to neutralise the arousal within minutes.

Unlike covert sensitisation (which creates aversion through disgust), imaginal desensitisation is less distressing — the client ends the imagined sequence feeling **calm and competent**, not disgusted. This may increase engagement and reduce dropout.

From a **cost perspective**: because sessions can be audio-recorded, the therapist only needs to spend significant time in the interview and script-writing phase. This makes it more accessible and cost-effective than extended 1-to-1 therapy.

---

**5. Strengths and Weaknesses**

**Strengths:**

✅ **Empirically supported by controlled trials (Grant et al., 2011)**
→ WHY: The technique has been tested in an RCT, not just case studies
→ CONSEQUENCE: 80% abstinence rate provides strong evidence of efficacy; comparison with Gamblers Anonymous shows it is significantly more effective than a common alternative (63.6% vs 17.1%)

✅ **Cost-effective and scalable**
→ WHY: Sessions can be recorded; therapist involvement is front-loaded in script writing
→ CONSEQUENCE: More patients can access therapy; reduces financial barriers; suitable for health services with limited budgets

✅ **Teaches transferable coping skills (PMR)**
→ WHY: PMR can be used by the client independently in triggering real-world situations
→ CONSEQUENCE: The client develops **self-efficacy** — a sense of personal control — rather than just avoiding triggers or relying on medication

✅ **Highly personalised**
→ WHY: Scripts are tailored to the individual's specific triggers, venues, emotional patterns
→ CONSEQUENCE: Higher ecological validity; the therapy mirrors the client's actual experience rather than a generic scenario

---

**Weaknesses:**

❌ **No outcome data for the Mary Doe case**
→ WHY: The case is used as a teaching illustration, not a formal study
→ CONSEQUENCE: Other therapists reading Blaszczynski and Nower (2003) learn how to conduct the technique but have no evidence it actually worked for Mary — limiting confidence in the specific application described

❌ **Requires deep relaxation ability**
→ WHY: If a client cannot achieve a state of deep relaxation (e.g., high anxiety, hyperarousal), the imagery sessions will not work effectively
→ CONSEQUENCE: Some clients may need anti-anxiety medication to participate effectively; this adds complexity and reduces accessibility

❌ **May not suit all client profiles**
→ WHY: Blaszczynski and Nower (2003) note it may be less effective for people with **attention deficit disorder** or **comorbid depression**
→ CONSEQUENCE: Clients who struggle with concentration or motivation may fail to complete daily homework sessions, reducing efficacy; that said, Grant et al. (2012) successfully used it with a 17-year-old with autism spectrum disorder and OCD, showing flexibility is possible

❌ **Requires high motivation and organisation**
→ WHY: Clients must complete 2–3 sessions of guided imagery per day for at least a week
→ CONSEQUENCE: Unmotivated or chaotic clients are unlikely to maintain the practice; this limits the population for whom the therapy is suitable

❌ **N = 1 case study (no control, no quantitative outcome for Mary)**
→ WHY: Case study methodology
→ CONSEQUENCE: Cannot establish causality; no comparison group; findings may not generalise — confounds (e.g., Mary stopping gambling for financial rather than therapeutic reasons) cannot be ruled out

---

**6. Exam tips**

⚡ **Must-knows:**
- Based on McConaghy's (1980) **Behaviour Completion Mechanism** — tension from incomplete sequences drives the urge
- 6-scene script structure: trigger → planning → arrival → arousal → second thoughts → walking away calmly
- Key components: **PMR** (progressive muscle relaxation) + **guided imagery** + **personalised script** + **homework** (2–3 times/day, 5–7 days)
- Only 2–3 face-to-face sessions; rest is audio-recorded homework
- No outcome data for Mary — weakness
- Grant et al. (2011): 80% abstinence, 77% at 6-month follow-up
- Grant et al. (2011) vs GA: 63.6% vs 17.1% abstinence
- Unlike covert sensitisation: ends with calm/relief, not disgust
- Weakness: requires motivation, relaxation ability, imagery capacity

📋 **Skeleton answer (describe, 6 marks):**
> Blaszczynski and Nower (2003) describe imaginal desensitisation as a relaxation-based psychological therapy for impulse control disorders, illustrated through the case of Mary Doe, a 52-year-old woman with gambling disorder. The therapist conducted an in-depth interview to identify the behavioural sequence leading to gambling, then wrote a personalised 6-scene guided imagery script. Sessions began with progressive muscle relaxation. The script guided Mary through her typical trigger chain, but ended with her leaving the casino calmly and choosing an alternative activity. Sessions were recorded and Mary practised the imagery 2–3 times daily as homework. The therapy aimed to extinguish conditioned arousal by pairing the trigger chain with deep relaxation rather than excitement.

📋 **Comparing biological vs psychological treatment (4 marks):**
> Both opiate antagonists (Grant et al., 2008) and imaginal desensitisation (Blaszczynski & Nower, 2003) aim to reduce the rewarding effects of impulse control behaviours. However, opiate antagonists achieve this biologically by blocking opioid receptors to prevent dopamine release, while imaginal desensitisation achieves it psychologically by extinguishing conditioned arousal through relaxation. Opiate antagonists require minimal daily effort (taking a pill) but do not build coping skills and may cause side effects. Imaginal desensitisation teaches transferable skills (PMR) but demands high motivation and daily practice.

---

---

## Quick Comparison Table: All Three Studies

| Feature | Grant et al. (2008) | Glover (1985) | Blaszczynski & Nower (2003) |
|---------|---------------------|---------------|------------------------------|
| **Type of treatment** | Biological (opiate antagonists) | Psychological (covert sensitisation) | Psychological (imaginal desensitisation) |
| **Disorder** | Gambling disorder | Kleptomania | Gambling disorder |
| **Design** | Meta-analysis of two RCTs | Single case study | Single case study (illustrative) |
| **N** | 284 | 1 | 1 |
| **Blinding** | Double-blind | None (case study) | None (case study) |
| **Duration** | 16–18 weeks | 4 sessions over 2 months | 2–3 sessions + daily homework |
| **Key finding** | Family history of alcoholism predicts drug response | 14-year stealing habit reduced in 2 months; sustained 19 months | Technique described; supported by Grant et al. (2011) — 80% abstinence |
| **Outcome data** | Strong (Y-BOCS, quantitative) | Yes (qualitative progress reports) | Not for Mary — illustrative only |
| **Main strength** | Double-blind RCT design | Long follow-up, rich case detail | Cost-effective, transferable skills |
| **Main weakness** | No follow-up after trial | N = 1; relies on imagery ability | No outcome data for the case described |
| **Approach** | Nomothetic | Idiographic | Idiographic |
| **Reductionist?** | Yes (biological only) | Partially (learning-based) | No (holistic — addresses triggers, cognitions, behaviour, emotion) |

---

## Key Terms Glossary

| Term | Definition |
|------|-----------|
| **Opiate antagonist** | Drug that blocks opioid receptors, preventing endorphin-driven dopamine release and reducing euphoria |
| **Nalmefene / Naltrexone** | Two specific opiate antagonists used in gambling disorder treatment |
| **Double-blind** | Neither participant nor researcher knows who is in the drug or placebo condition |
| **Placebo** | Inactive pill identical in appearance to the real drug; controls for expectation effects |
| **Y-BOCS** | Yale-Brown Obsessive-Compulsive Scale — used here to measure gambling symptom severity |
| **Covert sensitisation** | Therapy pairing mental images of unwanted behaviour with aversive imagery (e.g., vomiting) to create conditioned avoidance |
| **Imaginal desensitisation** | Therapy using relaxation + personalised scripts to extinguish conditioned arousal to gambling/stealing triggers |
| **Progressive Muscle Relaxation (PMR)** | Tensing then releasing muscle groups to achieve deep physiological relaxation |
| **Behaviour Completion Mechanism** | McConaghy's theory: incomplete behavioural sequences create tension that drives compulsive repetition |
| **Aphantasia** | Inability to form mental images — makes imagery-based therapies less effective |
| **Comorbid** | Having two or more diagnosable conditions at the same time |
| **Idiographic** | Focused on the individual; not aiming to generalise |
| **Nomothetic** | Focused on drawing universal conclusions that apply across a population |
| **SOGS** | South Oaks Gambling Screen — diagnostic/severity tool for gambling disorder |

---

- [Grant et al. (2011) — 6-month follow-up, PMC](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3465840/)
- [Glover (1985) — PubMed abstract](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/4052670/)
- [Kohn & Antonuccio (2002) — Clinical Case Studies](https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/abs/10.1177/1534650102001001003)
- [Blaszczynski & Nower (2003) — Imaginal Desensitisation guide](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/254381105_Imaginal_Desensitisation)
- [Opioid Antagonists for Gambling Disorder — PMC review](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6295935/)

# Studies: Anxiety & Fear-Related Disorders — Part 1 (6.4)

> Chapter 6.4 covers **Generalised Anxiety Disorder (GAD)**, **agoraphobia**, and **blood-injection-injury (BII) phobia**.
> Three formal studies/key studies are covered in this section:
> 1. **Spitzer et al. (2006)** — Development and validation of the GAD-7
> 2. **Mas et al. (2010)** — Development and validation of the Blood Injection Phobia Inventory (BIPI)
> 3. **Chapman and DeLapp (2013)** — CBT + Applied Tension treatment for BII phobia (case study)

---

## Quick-Reference Table

| Study | Disorder | Method | Key Finding |
|---|---|---|---|
| Spitzer et al. (2006) | GAD | Questionnaire + structured interview | GAD-7 is valid, reliable; r = +0.83 test-retest |
| Mas et al. (2010) | BII phobia | Questionnaire + matched controls | BIPI valid for Spanish speakers; Cronbach's α = .98 |
| Chapman & DeLapp (2013) | BII phobia | Single case study (CBT + AT) | 9 sessions produced near-complete recovery at 12-month follow-up |

---

## Study 1

### Spitzer et al. (2006) — Developing the GAD-7 scale to quickly screen for Generalised Anxiety Disorder

---

### 1. Why this study exists

**Generalised Anxiety Disorder (GAD)** affects roughly **2–5% of people** (up to 9% of women in some samples).
It is characterised by intense, persistent, **free-floating anxiety** — worry that attaches to almost anything: money, family, health, work.

Before 2006, no quick and precise screening tool for GAD existed for busy primary care settings.
Doctors needed something **short enough to use in a 10-minute appointment** but accurate enough to tell mild from severe cases.

> 💡 Think of it like a smoke alarm: it needs to be sensitive enough to detect real fires, but not so sensitive it goes off every time someone makes toast.

The researchers' goal was to **develop and validate** a seven-item questionnaire that could do exactly that.

---

### 2. The procedure — told as a story

**Setting the scene:**
It is 2004. You are a patient walking into one of **15 primary care clinics across Missouri, USA**.
Before you even see your doctor, you are handed a sheet of paper with questions.

**Step 1 — Building the item pool.**
The research team started with **13 candidate questions**, all based on the DSM-IV criteria for GAD and existing anxiety measures.
They covered things like: *"Are you feeling on edge? Are you worrying uncontrollably? Do you feel restless?"*

**Step 2 — Collecting data at scale.**
A total of **2,740 adult patients** completed the 13-item questionnaire.
Within **one week**, 965 of those patients also completed a **telephone interview** with a trained mental health professional — this was the gold-standard check against which the questionnaire was measured.

**Step 3 — Cutting it down.**
The researchers ran statistical analyses to find the **7 items** that best correlated with the full 13-item scale.
The weakest, most redundant questions were cut.

**Step 4 — Test-retest reliability check.**
Participants completed the questionnaire **twice, one week apart**.
This tested whether scores stayed consistent over time (i.e., was it measuring something stable?).

**Step 5 — Scoring the final tool.**
Each of the 7 items is rated on a **4-point scale**:

| Response | Score |
|---|---|
| Not at all | 0 |
| Several days | 1 |
| More than half the days | 2 |
| Nearly every day | 3 |

Maximum total score: **21 points**.

**Step 6 — Interpreting the score.**

| Score | Severity |
|---|---|
| 0–4 | Minimal/no GAD |
| 5–9 | **Mild GAD** |
| 10–14 | **Moderate GAD** → refer to psychiatrist |
| 15–21 | **Severe GAD** → prioritise urgently |

---

### 3. What they found

| Finding | Detail |
|---|---|
| Diagnosed GAD patients' average score | **14.4 / 21** |
| Non-diagnosed patients' average score | **4.9 / 21** |
| Test-retest reliability | **r = +0.83** (strong positive correlation) |
| Sensitivity (catching true cases) | **89%** |
| Specificity (correctly excluding non-cases) | **82%** |

> 🧠 A correlation of **+0.83** is very strong — scores barely changed from one week to the next, which means the tool measures something stable.

The GAD-7 also showed **strong concurrent validity** — its scores correlated highly with scores from other respected anxiety measures.

---

### 4. Why it matters

- Gives doctors a **fast, evidence-based screening tool** (takes ~2 minutes).
- Helps **prioritise** who needs urgent psychiatric referral vs. who can be monitored.
- **Prevents unnecessary referrals**, reducing waiting lists for those who genuinely need urgent help.
- Used globally in research AND clinical practice.
- The questionnaire was developed from a **huge sample (n = 2,740)**, making it highly generalisable.

---

### 5. Strengths and Weaknesses

**Strengths ✅**

✅ **Strong test-retest reliability (r = +0.83)**
→ WHY it matters: Scores taken one week apart were nearly identical.
→ CONSEQUENCE: Clinicians can trust that a score reflects the patient's actual stable anxiety level, not just a bad day.

✅ **Quick and practical**
→ Just 7 items, yet retains excellent validity.
→ CONSEQUENCE: Doctors with limited time can still gather clinically useful data to triage patients appropriately.

✅ **Large and diverse sample (n = 2,740)**
→ Drawn from 15 different clinics across Missouri.
→ CONSEQUENCE: Findings generalise more widely than studies with small, narrow samples.

✅ **Strong concurrent validity**
→ GAD-7 scores correlated strongly with established anxiety measures.
→ CONSEQUENCE: We can be confident the tool measures what it claims to measure (GAD severity).

**Weaknesses ❌**

❌ **Only two assessments, one week apart**
→ WHY this is a problem: GAD is diagnosed only after **several months** of symptoms.
→ CONSEQUENCE: The GAD-7 provides a **snapshot**, not a longitudinal picture. It cannot confirm whether symptoms are chronic or just a temporary reaction to a stressful event.

❌ **Self-report format — subjective**
→ Patients rate their own feelings.
→ CONSEQUENCE: Social desirability bias, mood on the day, or poor self-awareness may distort scores. Scores may not reflect objective symptom severity.

❌ **Sample from Missouri only**
→ Cultural, economic and demographic factors vary by region.
→ CONSEQUENCE: The cut-off scores may not be equally valid in other countries or cultures.

❌ **Gender imbalance in prevalence data**
→ Spitzer et al. found 9% prevalence in women vs. 4% in men.
→ CONSEQUENCE: If the scale was normed on a predominantly female sample, it may not perform equally well for men.

---

### 6. Exam tips

⚡ **Must-knows:**
- GAD-7 = **7 items**, scored **0–21**, developed from original pool of **13 items**
- n = **2,740** participants from **Missouri, USA**
- Test-retest reliability: **r = +0.83** (one week apart)
- Diagnosed GAD = avg **14.4**; controls = avg **4.9**
- Cut-offs: 5 = mild, 10 = moderate (refer), 15+ = severe (prioritise)
- Weakness: only a **snapshot**, not longitudinal; self-report = subjective

📋 **Skeleton exam answer (evaluate the GAD-7):**

> "A strength of the GAD-7 is its **test-retest reliability**. Spitzer et al. (2006) found a strong positive correlation of **r = +0.83** between scores taken one week apart. This is important because a reliable test gives consistent results, meaning clinicians can trust the score reflects a genuine stable condition rather than a transient mood state. However, a weakness is that the study only assessed participants **twice, one week apart**, rather than over several months. Since a GAD diagnosis requires symptoms persisting for several months, it is unclear whether a GAD-7 score accurately reflects the **chronicity** of symptoms over time."

---

## Study 2

### Mas et al. (2010) — Creating the Blood Injection Phobia Inventory (BIPI) for Spanish speakers

---

### 1. Why this study exists

**Blood-injection-injury (BII) phobia** affects roughly **3.5% of the population**.
It is unusual among phobias because — instead of just raising heart rate — it can cause people to **faint** (vasovagal syncope) through a two-stage biological response.

Before 2010, virtually all validated phobia assessment tools were in **English**.
Spanish-speaking populations had no reliable, culturally appropriate instrument.

> 💡 Imagine trying to diagnose colour-blindness using a test that only works under certain lighting conditions — if the tool doesn't fit the population, the diagnosis is meaningless.

Mas et al.'s aim: **develop and validate** a blood and injection phobia questionnaire specifically for **Spanish-speaking people**.

---

### 2. The procedure — told as a story

**Step 1 — Recruiting two groups.**
The researchers recruited participants from **Andalusia, Spain** (average age: 24).

- **Group A (phobic group):** **39 people** with a confirmed diagnosis of blood phobia.
- **Group B (control group):** **135 matched controls** — people without blood phobia, matched to Group A on age, marital status and educational level.

> 💡 Matching is like choosing a comparison group carefully so that any differences in scores can be attributed to the phobia, not to the fact that one group is older or better educated.

**Step 2 — The questionnaire.**
Both groups completed a large questionnaire containing **50 situations** related to:
- Agoraphobia (e.g., being in crowds)
- Social phobia (e.g., speaking in public)
- Blood-injection phobia (e.g., seeing an injured person bleed)

For each situation, participants rated how often they experienced **27 different reactions** across three categories:

| Category | Example response |
|---|---|
| **Cognitive** | "I think I'm going to faint." |
| **Biological** | "My palms sweat." |
| **Behavioural** | "My legs shake." |

Ratings used a **4-point Likert scale** (0 = never, 3 = always).

**Step 3 — The phobic group did extra work.**
Group A (the phobic group) also:
- Completed a **structured interview** about their phobia history.
- Completed the **Fear Questionnaire (FQ)** — an established English-language phobia measure — to provide a comparison benchmark.

**Step 4 — Cutting the questionnaire down.**
The researchers used **statistical analysis** to remove items:
- **10 items removed** first: these showed no significant difference between phobic and control groups (useless items).
- Further **factor analysis** applied: kept only the **18 situations** that best distinguished phobic from non-phobic individuals.

> 💡 Factor analysis is like a filter. Imagine you have 50 different alarm systems. Factor analysis finds which ones actually go off only when there's a burglar — and which ones go off randomly. You keep only the reliable ones.

**Final BIPI structure:**
- **18 situations** (stimuli)
- **27 phobic responses** to rate per situation
- Total: up to **486 data points per person**

---

### 3. What they found

| Finding | Detail |
|---|---|
| Internal reliability (Cronbach's α) | **0.98** — extremely high |
| Factor analysis | One dominant factor — blood phobia is a **unitary construct** |
| Factor 1 explains | **76%** of stimulus variance; **74%** of phobic response variance |
| Concurrent validity | Strong positive correlation between BIPI and blood sub-scale of the FQ |
| Group difference | Phobic group scored **significantly higher** than controls on the BIPI |

> 🧠 A Cronbach's alpha of **0.98** means the 18 items are extremely consistent with each other — they all measure the same underlying thing.

---

### 4. Why it matters

- Gives Spanish-speaking clinicians a **culturally valid** diagnostic tool.
- The BIPI's detail (27 reactions × 18 situations) lets therapists **tailor treatment** to the individual — e.g., is the patient more scared of their own blood or someone else's?
- Helps **identify** people with BII phobia who might otherwise avoid medical care and risk their health.
- Comparable to English-language tools, enabling cross-cultural research.

---

### 5. Strengths and Weaknesses

**Strengths ✅**

✅ **Strong concurrent validity**
→ BIPI scores correlated strongly with the blood sub-scale of the established Fear Questionnaire (FQ).
→ CONSEQUENCE: Clinicians can trust BIPI results translate meaningfully across languages and cultures.

✅ **Rigorous item selection using factor analysis**
→ Items were statistically tested, not chosen by guesswork.
→ CONSEQUENCE: Every item in the final BIPI earns its place — it adds diagnostic value and removes noise.

✅ **Comprehensive coverage**
→ 27 reactions × 18 situations = 486 data points per person.
→ CONSEQUENCE: Therapists get a detailed, individualised profile of each patient's specific fears and reactions.

✅ **Matched control group**
→ Controls matched on age, marital status and education.
→ CONSEQUENCE: Group differences in scores are more likely due to the phobia itself, not confounding variables.

**Weaknesses ❌**

❌ **Gender imbalance in phobic group**
→ 30 women vs. 9 men in the phobic group (3:1 ratio).
→ WHY it matters: Men were severely underrepresented.
→ CONSEQUENCE: The BIPI may be better calibrated for detecting phobia in women. Male patients may be under-identified.

❌ **Self-report data — subjective**
→ Participants rated hypothetical reactions ("how would you feel if...") rather than actual measured responses.
→ CONSEQUENCE: There may be a mismatch between imagined and real responses. People may underestimate or overestimate their reactions.

❌ **Sample limited to Andalusia, Spain**
→ All participants were from one Spanish region.
→ CONSEQUENCE: Findings may not generalise to all Spanish-speaking populations (e.g., Latin American cultures may differ).

❌ **Small phobic group (n = 39)**
→ Very small clinical sample compared to control group (n = 135).
→ CONSEQUENCE: Statistical power to detect differences is limited; results may not be stable if replicated.

---

### 6. Exam tips

⚡ **Must-knows:**
- BIPI = **18 situations** × **27 responses** = 486 data points
- Groups: **39 phobic** vs. **135 matched controls**, all from Andalusia, Spain
- Cronbach's α = **0.98** (excellent reliability)
- Factor analysis identified **one factor** explaining ~75% of variance
- Validated against the **Fear Questionnaire (FQ)**
- Key weakness: **gender imbalance** (30F : 9M in phobic group); self-report = subjective

📋 **Skeleton exam answer (evaluate the BIPI):**

> "A strength of the BIPI is its **concurrent validity**. Mas et al. (2010) found a strong positive correlation between BIPI scores and the blood sub-scale of the Fear Questionnaire (FQ). This is important because if two established measures of the same construct correlate strongly, it increases confidence that the BIPI is genuinely measuring blood-injection phobia. However, a weakness is the **gender imbalance**: the phobic group contained three times as many females (n=30) as males (n=9). This matters because the BIPI may be better at detecting phobia symptoms in women, meaning male patients with BII phobia could be under-identified in clinical settings."

---

## Study 3

### Chapman and DeLapp (2013) — Nine sessions of CBT + Applied Tension cured a 42-year-old man of severe BII phobia

---

### 1. Why this study exists

**Blood-injection-injury (BII) phobia** is not just unpleasant — it is dangerous.
People with BII phobia **avoid doctors, blood tests, vaccinations and medical procedures**.
Left untreated, this avoidance can cost lives.

BII phobia is harder to treat than most phobias because of the **diphasic (two-stage) biological response**:

| Stage | What happens |
|---|---|
| **Stage 1** | Heart rate and blood pressure **spike** (normal fear response) |
| **Stage 2 (vasovagal syncope)** | Blood pressure and heart rate **crash** → patient faints |

> 💡 It's like a car that revs its engine and then immediately stalls. The crash in blood pressure cuts oxygen to the brain — unconsciousness follows.

Standard exposure therapy (face your fear gradually) doesn't work if the patient **keeps fainting** during it — you can't learn that blood is safe if you keep blacking out.

Chapman and DeLapp (2013) wanted to show how **Cognitive Behavioural Therapy (CBT) combined with Applied Tension (AT)** — a technique that prevents fainting — could successfully treat a severe, long-standing BII phobia.

---

### 2. The procedure — told as a story

**Meet T.**
T is a **42-year-old Hispanic male**. For over **20 years**, he has been terrified of medical situations.
He faints during blood tests. He perceives doctors' waiting rooms as threatening.
His child has **autism spectrum disorder** and needs regular medical appointments — but T cannot go with him.
He feels guilt and shame. He has not seen a regular doctor in years (only a psychiatrist for anti-anxiety medication).
He exercises obsessively (running, cycling, weight training, swimming) to feel in control of his body.

> 🧠 T's history includes early vulnerability: his mother joked about his heart problems, his grandmother listened to ambulance scanners daily, and he witnessed the deaths of his grandfather, uncle and aunt from illness. Nurture, not just nature, shaped his phobia.

**Method: Single case study.**
Data collected using:
- **Quantitative:** self-report questionnaires (BAI, FSS-II, BISS, SUDS scores during sessions)
- **Qualitative:** diagnostic interview; T's verbal comments throughout treatment

**Baseline assessment (before treatment):**

| Measure | What it measures | T's baseline score |
|---|---|---|
| Beck Anxiety Inventory (BAI) | General anxiety (0–63) | **41 — severe anxiety** |
| Fear Survey Schedule II (FSS-II) | Fear of 51 objects/situations (7-point scale) | **6 ("terror")** for blood; **5 ("very much fear")** for needles, death, illness |
| Blood-Injection Symptom Scale (BISS) | 17 physical/psychological symptoms | **All 17 items endorsed** |

**The treatment: 9 sessions over 9 weeks.**

> 💡 Think of the 9 sessions as a climbing wall. T starts on the easiest holds at the bottom (just looking at a photo of a needle) and slowly works his way up to the hardest holds at the top (getting his own blood drawn at a clinic).

**Sessions 1–3: Build the foundation**
- **Psychoeducation:** T learns what BII phobia is, why he faints (diphasic response), what vasovagal syncope is.
- **Self-monitoring:** T starts completing the **Phobic Encounter Record (PER)** between sessions — every time he faces blood/medical stimuli, he writes down his thoughts, feelings and anxiety score (0–100, called the **SUDS — Subjective Units of Discomfort Scale**).
- **Cognitive restructuring:** T identifies **thinking errors** (e.g., "medical procedures are dangerous") and learns to challenge them.
- **Progress note:** T uses his new knowledge at the hairdresser — he feels panicky but stays seated, challenges his thoughts and calms down. First win.

**Session 4: Build the fear ladder**
- T creates a **10-item fear hierarchy**, each item rated 0–100 for distress:
  - Lowest: reading about blood tests
  - Highest: having his own blood drawn at a clinic
- The therapist explains SUDS scoring.
- **Progress note:** T feels optimistic. He buys a blood pressure cuff and a heart rate monitor to prepare himself.

**Session 5: Learn Applied Tension**
- The therapist **explains and demonstrates Applied Tension (AT)**:

> **Applied Tension (AT) — step by step:**
> 1. Sit in a comfortable chair.
> 2. Tense the muscles of your **arms, torso and legs** simultaneously.
> 3. Hold the tension for **10–15 seconds**.
> 4. Release for **20–30 seconds**.
> 5. Repeat **five times**.
>
> 💡 Tensing large muscle groups **raises blood pressure**, counteracting the vasovagal crash that causes fainting. It is the physiological opposite of fainting.

- T is asked to practise AT **five times a day** at home.
- **Progress note:** T hasn't faced phobic situations yet but feels more aware of early fainting cues.

**Session 6: First real exposure**
- In session: T watches **YouTube videos of people having blood tests**.
  - SUDS starts at 30, rises to 35 at the moment blood enters the tube.
  - He uses AT. SUDS stabilises at 35, then drops to 20.
  - Says: *"Not bad... slight nausea but not a big deal."*
  - Closer camera angle videos: similar SUDS.
- **Progress note (homework):** T completed item 1 on his hierarchy at home — SUDS was 40 but said *"it was fine."* He called this an *"immense success."*

**Session 7: His own blood**
- In session: T **pricks his finger** to check blood sugar and looks at his own blood.
  - SUDS before: 45. Drops to 20. No AT needed after first prick.
  - Two more finger pricks: SUDS stays at 20. He says *"about there."*
- **Progress note (homework):** T's wife checks his blood sugar three times using the finger-prick test.
  - SUDS = 45; used AT for first two tests; did the third without AT.
  - Looked at his blood for **five minutes**.

**Session 8: Watching blood donors**
- In session: T and therapist visit a **blood bank** (donation centre).
  - SUDS before: 65. Drops to 45 within 5 minutes, then to 30 within 10 minutes.
  - No AT used. No fainting.
  - Watches a **second donor**: SUDS = 0.
- T agrees to book his own blood test for the coming week.
- **Progress note:** T makes the appointment.

**Session 9: Blood pressure and solo exposure**
- In session: T takes his own blood pressure.
  - SUDS = 55, drops to 20. Second measurement: SUDS stays at 20.
- Plans to book a blood pressure test with a nurse during the week.
- **Progress note (between sessions):** T **has his own blood drawn at a clinic**.
  - Used AT only at the start. SUDS = 40, drops quickly to 0.
  - Second blood draw from other arm: SUDS peaks at 25.

**After session 9:**
T takes his blood pressure at a pharmacy. A nurse takes it. A doctor examines him.
No AT needed. SUDS: *"rather low."*
T says: **"I never felt better in my life."**

**Follow-up:**

| Time point | Outcome |
|---|---|
| **4 months post-treatment** | T thanks therapist; multiple doctor's appointments booked |
| **10 months post-treatment** | Still doing well |
| **12 months post-treatment** | Completes follow-up questionnaires |

**12-month follow-up scores vs. baseline:**

| Measure | Baseline | 12-month follow-up |
|---|---|---|
| BAI (anxiety) | **41 (severe)** | **7 (low)** |
| FSS-II (blood) | **6 ("terror")** | No items rated "very much fear" or "terror" |
| BISS (symptoms) | **17/17 endorsed** | **4/17 symptoms** (anxious, heart pounding, nauseous, sweating) |

---

### 3. What they found

> T went from **severe, 20-year BII phobia** — unable to attend his child's medical appointments — to **voluntarily booking his own doctor's visits** within 9 weeks.

- SUDS scores **consistently declined** across sessions and tasks.
- Qualitative data showed T moved from terror to confidence.
- At 12-month follow-up, **all major anxiety measures had dropped dramatically**.
- The combination of **psychoeducation + cognitive restructuring + graduated exposure + applied tension** was effective for a severe, complex case.

---

### 4. Why it matters

- Demonstrates CBT+AT can work for **severe, long-standing BII phobia** (20+ years).
- Shows treatment is possible in **outpatient community settings** — not just specialised hospital clinics.
- Highlights the importance of **applied tension** as a unique addition to standard CBT for BII (unlike other phobias, fainting is a real barrier to exposure).
- Provides a **detailed clinical roadmap** other therapists can adapt for their own clients.
- Improved T's **quality of life** and potentially saved his life by enabling routine medical check-ups.

---

### 5. Strengths and Weaknesses

**Strengths ✅**

✅ **Triangulation of data (quantitative + qualitative)**
→ SUDS scores tracked anxiety numerically across sessions; interviews captured T's subjective experience.
→ CONSEQUENCE: The combination increases the **credibility** of the findings — the numbers and the words tell the same story.

✅ **Detailed case history provided**
→ T's full background (family history, depression, OCD, social anxiety, panic disorder) is documented.
→ CONSEQUENCE: Clinicians reading the study can judge for themselves whether T's profile matches their own clients, helping them decide whether CBT+AT is appropriate for a given patient. This supports **transferability** despite the lack of a control group.

✅ **Longitudinal follow-up (12 months)**
→ Data collected at baseline, throughout treatment and 12 months later.
→ CONSEQUENCE: Shows the treatment had **lasting** effects, not just short-term improvements that faded.

✅ **Ethically strong**
→ T's anonymity protected (referred to only as "T").
→ He set the pace of exposure — nothing was forced.
→ CONSEQUENCE: Minimal psychological harm; beneficence demonstrated.

**Weaknesses ❌**

❌ **Single case study (n = 1) — cannot generalise**
→ Only one patient treated by one experienced therapist.
→ WHY it matters: We cannot know if results would replicate with a different patient, a different therapist or a different setting.
→ CONSEQUENCE: Findings have **low external validity** — the therapy may not work as well for everyone.

❌ **No control group**
→ There is no comparison group (e.g., a sham-treatment group).
→ WHY it matters: We cannot rule out that T recovered due to **expectancy effects** (believing he would get better) or natural remission rather than the therapy itself.
→ CONSEQUENCE: Cause and effect cannot be firmly established.

❌ **Self-reported SUDS scores — potentially invalid**
→ T rated his own distress aloud, in the presence of his therapist.
→ WHY it matters: T had **social anxiety** — he may have reported lower scores than he genuinely felt to please the therapist or avoid embarrassment.
→ CONSEQUENCE: The quantitative data may not accurately reflect T's genuine anxiety levels, inflating the apparent success of the treatment.

❌ **Idiographic, not nomothetic**
→ The study provides rich detail about one person but no group-level statistics.
→ WHY it matters: Average SUDS scores across a large sample would allow us to understand individual differences in treatment efficacy.
→ CONSEQUENCE: It is impossible to know whether T's exceptional outcome is typical or an outlier.

❌ **Practical barriers to access**
→ T had to travel **2.5 hours** each way for sessions.
→ WHY it matters: Most patients cannot access specialist BII clinics, especially in rural areas.
→ CONSEQUENCE: The treatment model shown here may not be replicable for many patients due to geographic and financial barriers.

---

### 6. Exam tips

⚡ **Must-knows:**
- T = 42-year-old Hispanic male, **20+ years** of BII phobia
- Method = **single case study**; collected quantitative (SUDS, BAI, FSS-II, BISS) + qualitative (interview, verbal comments)
- **9 sessions** using the *Mastering Your Fears and Phobias (MYFP)* manual
- **Diphasic response**: heart rate/BP spike, then crash (vasovagal syncope = fainting)
- **Applied Tension**: tense major muscle groups for 10–15 sec, release 20–30 sec, repeat 5× → raises BP → prevents fainting
- **Fear hierarchy**: 10 items, rated 0–100 SUDS; sessions moved from videos of blood tests → watching blood donors → giving own blood
- BAI: **41 (baseline) → 7 (12 months)**
- Key strengths: triangulation, detailed case history, 12-month follow-up
- Key weaknesses: no control group, n=1 (idiographic, can't generalise), self-reported SUDS (social desirability bias), practical barriers to access

📋 **Skeleton exam answer (evaluate Chapman & DeLapp, 2013):**

> "A strength of Chapman and DeLapp (2013) is the use of **triangulation** — both quantitative SUDS scores and qualitative interview data were collected. This is important because the two types of data corroborate each other, increasing the credibility of the findings. However, a critical weakness is the **absence of a control group**. Without a comparison group (e.g., a sham-treatment group or a waitlist control), it is impossible to determine whether T's improvement was due to the CBT+AT programme specifically, or simply the result of expectancy effects and his personal motivation to change his behaviour for the sake of his family. This limits the **internal validity** of the study."

> "A further weakness is the **idiographic** nature of the design: with only one participant, findings cannot be generalised (nomothetic). A larger-scale RCT including multiple patients would allow researchers to calculate average improvement scores and quantify how much individual differences affect treatment outcomes."

---

## Cross-Study Connections

| Theme | GAD-7 (Spitzer 2006) | BIPI (Mas 2010) | CBT+AT (Chapman & DeLapp 2013) |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Disorder** | GAD | BII phobia | BII phobia |
| **Purpose** | Screening/diagnosis | Screening/diagnosis | Treatment |
| **Method** | Large-scale questionnaire | Matched-groups questionnaire | Single case study |
| **Data type** | Quantitative | Quantitative | Mixed (quant + qual) |
| **Key strength** | Test-retest reliability | Factor analysis rigor | Longitudinal follow-up |
| **Key weakness** | Snapshot only (no longitudinal) | Gender imbalance | N=1, no control group |
| **Generalisability** | High (n=2,740) | Moderate (n=174) | Low (n=1) |

---

## Key Terms — Quick Reference Glossary

| Term | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **GAD** | Generalised Anxiety Disorder — persistent, excessive anxiety about everyday things |
| **Free-floating anxiety** | Anxiety not attached to a specific cause; "general dread" |
| **GAD-7** | 7-item questionnaire scoring 0–21 to screen for GAD severity |
| **Test-retest reliability** | Consistency of scores when the same test is repeated after a short interval |
| **Concurrent validity** | A test is valid if it correlates strongly with another established measure of the same thing |
| **BII phobia** | Blood-Injection-Injury phobia — irrational fear of blood, needles and injury |
| **Diphasic response** | Two-stage biological reaction in BII: first BP rises, then crashes → fainting |
| **Vasovagal syncope** | Fainting caused by sudden drop in heart rate and blood pressure |
| **Applied Tension (AT)** | Tensing major muscle groups to raise blood pressure and prevent fainting during exposure |
| **BIPI** | Blood Injection Phobia Inventory — 18-situation × 27-response assessment tool for Spanish speakers |
| **Likert scale** | Rating scale with numbered points (e.g., 0–3 or 0–6) |
| **Factor analysis** | Statistical technique that identifies which items cluster together measuring the same underlying construct |
| **Cronbach's alpha** | Measure of internal consistency (reliability); 0.98 = near-perfect |
| **SUDS** | Subjective Units of Discomfort Scale — 0–100 anxiety self-rating used during exposure |
| **Graduated exposure** | Systematic, step-by-step confrontation of feared stimuli, from least to most feared |
| **Fear hierarchy** | Ordered list of feared situations from least to most distressing |
| **Idiographic** | Focusing on the individual (e.g., case study) |
| **Nomothetic** | Focusing on groups to establish general laws |
| **Psychoeducation** | Teaching a patient about their disorder and treatment rationale |
| **Cognitive restructuring** | Identifying and challenging irrational/unhelpful thoughts |
| **Agoraphobia** | Fear and avoidance of situations where escape seems difficult or help unavailable |
| **Animal reminder disgust** | Repulsion triggered by reminders of our physical, animal nature (e.g., blood, veins) — linked to fainting in BII |

---

- [Spitzer et al. (2006) — GAD-7 original paper (PubMed)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16717171/)
- [Mas et al. (2010) — BIPI development (Anales de Psicología)](https://revistas.um.es/analesps/article/view/91971)
- [Chapman & DeLapp (2013) — CBT+AT case study (Clinical Case Studies)](https://drkevinchapman.com/wp-content/uploads/2017/01/ChapmanDeLapp_BII-Phobia-preprint.pdf)
- [Generalized Anxiety Disorder 7 — Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generalized_Anxiety_Disorder_7)

> This file covers ALL formal studies from Chapter 6.4 (Anxiety & Fear-Related Disorders) and
> Chapter 6.5 (OCD — Diagnosis, Explanations & Treatment), spanning the textbook pages 308–349.
> Each study is explained as a story with full evaluation and exam guidance.
